Yeah, hi. Thanks. Um, I'm here because... well, ever since they made me the acting workflow lead at the warehouse, things have just been... a lot. I mean, I walk in the door and I'm already tense, you know? It's like my shoulders are up by my ears before I even clock in. We use this system, SAP, and lately, I've just been snapping over the smallest errors in it. Which is weird because my buddy Dave—he works over in receiving—he always complains about SAP, but he never gets mad about it. He actually bought this old Mustang he’s fixing up, and he was telling me the other day about the carburetor... anyway, I'm just missing cycle-count deadlines that I never used to miss. And then I go home and I'm just totally drained. I don't even have the energy to play basketball with the guys on Tuesdays anymore. Or work on my sketches. I used to sketch a lot of, like, architectural stuff. Bridges, mostly. I really like the suspension cables on the Golden Gate, how they... but yeah, I just don't do any of that now. I'm just too tired.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Um... down or sad most of the time? I mean, I don't really think so. Like I said before, I don't feel like I'm walking around crying or anything. It's not like a constant sadness. It's just... I don't know, it's more like a heaviness, I guess? But only really when I'm thinking about the warehouse. 

Speaking of the warehouse, they just changed the whole inventory system on us again. It's this new software, supposed to be cloud-based or whatever. But the tablets they gave us to use it on are so old, the battery dies in like two hours. So you're constantly having to plug it in by the loading dock, and the cord is super short, so you have to stand there holding it while you scan the barcodes. And the barcodes themselves get all smudged from the forklift exhaust, so half the time the scanner won't even read them. You have to type in this twelve-digit SKU manually, and if you mess up one number, it throws off the whole manifest. My supervisor, Greg, he's always on my case about the scan rates, but I'm like, "Greg, if you gave us equipment from this decade, maybe we could actually hit the numbers." He just shrugs. He's one of those guys who wears his sunglasses on the back of his neck, you know? Like, indoors. 

But, uh... wait. Sad most of the time. No, I wouldn't say that. If work isn't on my mind, I'm not really sad. I'm just kind of... there. Like I'm just waiting for the next shift to start so I can get yelled at by Greg again.

It's this new software, supposed to be cloud-based or whatever. But the tablets they gave us to use it on are so old, the battery dies in like two hours. And the barcodes themselves get all smudged from the forklift exhaust, so half the time the scanner won't even read them. You have to type in this twelve-digit SKU manually, and if you mess up one number, it throws off the whole manifest. He's one of those guys who wears his sunglasses on the back of his neck, you know? Like, indoors. \n\nBut, uh... wait. Sad most of the time. No, I wouldn't say that. If work isn't on my mind, I'm not really sad. I'm just kind of... there. Like I'm just waiting for the next shift to start so I can get yelled at by Greg again.\n\n[Last Question]\nCan you recall a time recently when you felt genuinely happy or satisfied, even for a little while, when work wasn’t on your mind?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Um... genuinely happy or satisfied? I mean... I don't really know. I guess I haven't really been keeping track of, like, happy moments. It's hard to think of one right off the top of my head. 

I did find this old watch in a drawer the other day, though. It was my grandfather's, one of those mechanical ones you have to wind every day. It's got a leather strap that's practically falling apart, but the face is still in really good shape. It's a Bulova, I think from the sixties? I took it to this little repair shop downtown—it's tucked between a dry cleaner and a place that sells, like, discount vape juice. The guy in there is ancient, he wears these crazy magnifying glasses strapped to his head. He told me the mainspring was shot, but he could fix it if he could find the part. He was telling me how nobody makes watches like that anymore, how everything is just cheap quartz movements now. He actually showed me the inside of an Apple Watch he was trying to fix for someone, and it's literally just a tiny computer board glued to a screen. There's no craftsmanship, you know? 

But, uh... what were you asking? Oh, feeling happy. Yeah, I don't really have a good answer for that. I can't really think of a specific time recently. It's just all kind of a blur.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Um... a moment of pleasure. Like a good cup of coffee. I mean, honestly, no, I can't really think of one. I wish I could. 

It's funny you mention coffee, though, because the coffee in our breakroom is just an absolute nightmare. They buy these giant, industrial-sized plastic tubs of the cheapest grounds possible, and it always tastes like burnt dirt. And the machine itself... I swear it hasn't been cleaned since maybe 2018. If you open the top lid where the water goes, there's this thick white crust built up all over the spray head. I actually pointed it out to Ron the other day. He's the facility manager. Ron is one of those guys who constantly walks around the warehouse holding a clipboard just to look busy, but I have literally never seen him write a single thing on it. He just taps his pen against the clip while you talk to him. Anyway, I told him the coffee maker is probably a massive health hazard, and he just gave me this long speech about the quarterly budget. Like, the company can afford to switch our entire inventory system to that broken cloud software I was telling you about, but they can't spend forty bucks on a new Mr. Coffee at Target? It makes zero sense. 

But, uh... wait. Did you ask about feeling satisfied? Yeah, sorry. No, I haven't really felt that way. Even with small stuff, it just hasn't really happened.

Um, I'm here because... well, ever since they made me the acting workflow lead at the warehouse, things have just been... a lot. I mean, I walk in the door and I'm already tense, you know? It's like my shoulders are up by my ears before I even clock in. We use this system, SAP, and lately, I've just been snapping over the smallest errors in it. Which is weird because my buddy Dave—he works over in receiving—he always complains about SAP, but he never gets mad about it. He actually bought this old Mustang he’s fixing up, and he was telling me the other day about the carburetor... anyway, I'm just missing cycle-count deadlines that I never used to miss. And then I go home and I'm just totally drained. I don't even have the energy to play basketball with the guys on Tuesdays anymore. Or work on my sketches. I used to sketch a lot of, like, architectural stuff. Bridges, mostly. I really like the suspension cables on the Golden Gate, how they... but yeah, I just don't do any of that now. I'm just too tired.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned you used to enjoy sketching, especially architectural things like bridges. Have you completely lost interest in doing that, or do you still want to but just feel too tired?\n\nPatient: I mean... I don't really know. I guess I still want to? Sometimes I look at my sketchpad and just... I don't know. I have this really nice set of graphite pencils, actually. Faber-Castell. My sister got them for me a few years back. She lives out in Denver now, she works at this massive brewery out there. They make this stout that tastes like peanut butter, which sounds totally gross, right? But it's actually pretty good, especially when it's cold out. \n\nBut anyway, she sent me those pencils and I keep them in this little wooden box on my desk. And lately I just go home, sit down, look at the box, and... I don't even open it. I just end up going to bed. So I'm not really sure if the interest is gone or if my brain is just completely fried from the warehouse.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe your mood throughout the day, especially when you’re not at work or thinking about work? Do you feel down or sad even when there’s no specific reason for it?\n\nPatient: Um... my mood when I'm not at work? I don't really know if I feel sad, exactly. Like, if there's no reason for it, I don't think I'm just walking around crying or anything. It's more just... I don't know, I'm always kind of thinking about the warehouse anyway. \n\nLike, even this past Sunday, I was trying to just chill out and not think about it. I went to Home Depot to get some weather stripping for my back door because the draft in my kitchen is getting ridiculous. My house was built in the 70s, and I swear the insulation is just garbage. You know those houses with the weird, dark wood paneling everywhere? Yeah, my whole basement is covered in it. I've been meaning to rip it out for years, maybe put up some drywall, paint it... I don't know, a light grey or something just to open the space up. But drywall is so expensive right now. I saw the prices in the lumber aisle and almost walked right out. The guy working there was zero help, too, he just kept looking at his phone. \n\nBut, um... wait, what were you asking? Oh, feeling down. Yeah, I haven't really noticed feeling sad for no specific reason. I just feel kind of blank when I get home.\n\nDoctor: When you’re not at work, do you still find enjoyment in other things, like hanging out with friends, watching TV, or even small things like a good meal or a comfortable bed?\n\nPatient: Um... enjoyment in other things? I mean, I guess I still like a good meal. Like, there's this taco truck that parks near the warehouse on Thursdays. Best al pastor I've ever had. They have this green salsa that is just... man, it's got a kick. The guy who runs it, Hector, he's always telling me I need to try making it at home. He says the secret is roasting the tomatillos until they're almost burnt. I actually bought a cast-iron skillet a few months ago thinking I'd try it. You know, one of those heavy Lodge ones? They say you're not supposed to use soap on them, just scrub them with coarse salt, which sounds kind of unsanitary if you think about it. But my buddy Dave swears by it. He uses his for everything, even baking cornbread. I don't really get the appeal of cornbread, honestly. It's too dry. \n\nBut, uh, anyway... hanging out with friends? Like I said, I haven't been playing basketball. I don't really watch much TV either. I just kind of sit there. So, I don't know. I guess I'm not really finding a lot of enjoyment in stuff right now. Just the tacos, maybe.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you don't enjoy things as much as you used to, even things you really liked before, like sketching or hanging out with friends?\n\nPatient: I mean, yeah, I guess I just said that, right? I'm not really enjoying much of anything lately. It's weird, because I used to be able to just zone out with my sketchbook for hours. I'd go down to the riverfront... they just put in this new pedestrian bridge down there a couple years ago. It's supposed to look like a suspension bridge, but it's really just cable-stayed, you know? The city council made a huge deal about it, spent way too much money on the lighting design. They have these LED strips that change colors on holidays. It was green for St. Patrick's Day, which was kind of cool, but then they left it green for like a week after, and it just looked like a giant glowing glowstick across the water. \n\nBut, um... yeah, I don't know. I just don't have the energy for it. Even when Dave texts me about shooting hoops or grabbing a beer, I just tell him I'm busy. I'm not really busy, I just... I don't want to go. It just feels like too much effort to even pretend I'm having a good time.\n\nDoctor: Would you say you're feeling down or sad most of the time now, even when things aren't stressful at work?\n\nPatient: Um... down or sad most of the time? I mean, I don't really think so. Like I said before, I don't feel like I'm walking around crying or anything. It's not like a constant sadness. It's just... I don't know, it's more like a heaviness, I guess? But only really when I'm thinking about the warehouse. \n\nSpeaking of the warehouse, they just changed the whole inventory system on us again. It's this new software, supposed to be cloud-based or whatever. But the tablets they gave us to use it on are so old, the battery dies in like two hours. So you're constantly having to plug it in by the loading dock, and the cord is super short, so you have to stand there holding it while you scan the barcodes. And the barcodes themselves get all smudged from the forklift exhaust, so half the time the scanner won't even read them. You have to type in this twelve-digit SKU manually, and if you mess up one number, it throws off the whole manifest. My supervisor, Greg, he's always on my case about the scan rates, but I'm like, \"Greg, if you gave us equipment from this decade, maybe we could actually hit the numbers.\" He just shrugs. He's one of those guys who wears his sunglasses on the back of his neck, you know? Like, indoors. \n\nBut, uh... wait. Sad most of the time. No, I wouldn't say that. If work isn't on my mind, I'm not really sad. I'm just kind of... there. Like I'm just waiting for the next shift to start so I can get yelled at by Greg again.\n\nDoctor: Can you recall a time recently when you felt genuinely happy or satisfied, even for a little while, when work wasn’t on your mind?\n\nPatient: Um... genuinely happy or satisfied? I mean... I don't really know. I guess I haven't really been keeping track of, like, happy moments. It's hard to think of one right off the top of my head. \n\nI did find this old watch in a drawer the other day, though. It was my grandfather's, one of those mechanical ones you have to wind every day. It's got a leather strap that's practically falling apart, but the face is still in really good shape. It's a Bulova, I think from the sixties? I took it to this little repair shop downtown—it's tucked between a dry cleaner and a place that sells, like, discount vape juice. The guy in there is ancient, he wears these crazy magnifying glasses strapped to his head. He told me the mainspring was shot, but he could fix it if he could find the part. He was telling me how nobody makes watches like that anymore, how everything is just cheap quartz movements now. He actually showed me the inside of an Apple Watch he was trying to fix for someone, and it's literally just a tiny computer board glued to a screen. There's no craftsmanship, you know? \n\nBut, uh... what were you asking? Oh, feeling happy. Yeah, I don't really have a good answer for that. I can't really think of a specific time recently. It's just all kind of a blur.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any moment in the past few weeks where you felt even a little bit of pleasure or satisfaction, even if it was something small, like finishing a task or having a good cup of coffee?\n\nPatient: Um... a moment of pleasure. Like a good cup of coffee. I mean, honestly, no, I can't really think of one. I wish I could. \n\nIt's funny you mention coffee, though, because the coffee in our breakroom is just an absolute nightmare. They buy these giant, industrial-sized plastic tubs of the cheapest grounds possible, and it always tastes like burnt dirt. And the machine itself... I swear it hasn't been cleaned since maybe 2018. Like, the company can afford to switch our entire inventory system to that broken cloud software I was telling you about, but they can't spend forty bucks on a new Mr. Coffee at Target? Um... pleasure or enjoyment in something I used to like? I don't... I don't think so. Like, I used to really like working on my car, you know? Just tinkering with it on the weekends. But I haven't even opened the hood in weeks. 

Speaking of my car, though, it's making this really weird sound when I turn the wheel. They brought in these new automated pallet jacks, and suddenly they don't need as many of us on the floor. It's just ridiculous.

But it's actually pretty good, especially when it's cold out. \n\nBut anyway, she sent me those pencils and I keep them in this little wooden box on my desk. And lately I just go home, sit down, look at the box, and... I don't even open it. I just end up going to bed. So I'm not really sure if the interest is gone or if my brain is just completely fried from the warehouse.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe your mood throughout the day, especially when you’re not at work or thinking about work? Do you feel down or sad even when there’s no specific reason for it?\n\nPatient: Um... my mood when I'm not at work? I don't really know if I feel sad, exactly. Like, if there's no reason for it, I don't think I'm just walking around crying or anything. It's more just... I don't know, I'm always kind of thinking about the warehouse anyway. \n\nLike, even this past Sunday, I was trying to just chill out and not think about it. I went to Home Depot to get some weather stripping for my back door because the draft in my kitchen is getting ridiculous. My house was built in the 70s, and I swear the insulation is just garbage. You know those houses with the weird, dark wood paneling everywhere? Yeah, my whole basement is covered in it. I've been meaning to rip it out for years, maybe put up some drywall, paint it... I don't know, a light grey or something just to open the space up. But drywall is so expensive right now. I saw the prices in the lumber aisle and almost walked right out. The guy working there was zero help, too, he just kept looking at his phone. \n\nBut, um... wait, what were you asking? Oh, feeling down. Yeah, I haven't really noticed feeling sad for no specific reason. I just feel kind of blank when I get home.\n\nDoctor: When you’re not at work, do you still find enjoyment in other things, like hanging out with friends, watching TV, or even small things like a good meal or a comfortable bed?\n\nPatient: Um... enjoyment in other things? I mean, I guess I still like a good meal. Like, there's this taco truck that parks near the warehouse on Thursdays. Best al pastor I've ever had. They have this green salsa that is just... man, it's got a kick. The guy who runs it, Hector, he's always telling me I need to try making it at home. He says the secret is roasting the tomatillos until they're almost burnt. I actually bought a cast-iron skillet a few months ago thinking I'd try it. You know, one of those heavy Lodge ones? They say you're not supposed to use soap on them, just scrub them with coarse salt, which sounds kind of unsanitary if you think about it. But my buddy Dave swears by it. He uses his for everything, even baking cornbread. I don't really get the appeal of cornbread, honestly. It's too dry. \n\nBut, uh, anyway... hanging out with friends? Like I said, I haven't been playing basketball. I don't really watch much TV either. I just kind of sit there. So, I don't know. I guess I'm not really finding a lot of enjoyment in stuff right now. Just the tacos, maybe.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you don't enjoy things as much as you used to, even things you really liked before, like sketching or hanging out with friends?\n\nPatient: I mean, yeah, I guess I just said that, right? I'm not really enjoying much of anything lately. It's weird, because I used to be able to just zone out with my sketchbook for hours. I'd go down to the riverfront... they just put in this new pedestrian bridge down there a couple years ago. It's supposed to look like a suspension bridge, but it's really just cable-stayed, you know? The city council made a huge deal about it, spent way too much money on the lighting design. They have these LED strips that change colors on holidays. It was green for St. Patrick's Day, which was kind of cool, but then they left it green for like a week after, and it just looked like a giant glowing glowstick across the water. \n\nBut, um... yeah, I don't know. I just don't have the energy for it. Even when Dave texts me about shooting hoops or grabbing a beer, I just tell him I'm busy. I'm not really busy, I just... I don't want to go. It just feels like too much effort to even pretend I'm having a good time.\n\nDoctor: Would you say you're feeling down or sad most of the time now, even when things aren't stressful at work?\n\nPatient: Um... down or sad most of the time? I mean, I don't really think so. Like I said before, I don't feel like I'm walking around crying or anything. It's not like a constant sadness. It's just... I don't know, it's more like a heaviness, I guess? But only really when I'm thinking about the warehouse. \n\nSpeaking of the warehouse, they just changed the whole inventory system on us again. It's this new software, supposed to be cloud-based or whatever. But the tablets they gave us to use it on are so old, the battery dies in like two hours. So you're constantly having to plug it in by the loading dock, and the cord is super short, so you have to stand there holding it while you scan the barcodes. And the barcodes themselves get all smudged from the forklift exhaust, so half the time the scanner won't even read them. You have to type in this twelve-digit SKU manually, and if you mess up one number, it throws off the whole manifest. My supervisor, Greg, he's always on my case about the scan rates, but I'm like, \"Greg, if you gave us equipment from this decade, maybe we could actually hit the numbers.\" He just shrugs. He's one of those guys who wears his sunglasses on the back of his neck, you know? Like, indoors. \n\nBut, uh... wait. Sad most of the time. No, I wouldn't say that. If work isn't on my mind, I'm not really sad. I'm just kind of... there. Like I'm just waiting for the next shift to start so I can get yelled at by Greg again.\n\nDoctor: Can you recall a time recently when you felt genuinely happy or satisfied, even for a little while, when work wasn’t on your mind?\n\nPatient: Um... genuinely happy or satisfied? I mean... I don't really know. I guess I haven't really been keeping track of, like, happy moments. It's hard to think of one right off the top of my head. \n\nI did find this old watch in a drawer the other day, though. It was my grandfather's, one of those mechanical ones you have to wind every day. It's got a leather strap that's practically falling apart, but the face is still in really good shape. It's a Bulova, I think from the sixties? I took it to this little repair shop downtown—it's tucked between a dry cleaner and a place that sells, like, discount vape juice. The guy in there is ancient, he wears these crazy magnifying glasses strapped to his head. He told me the mainspring was shot, but he could fix it if he could find the part. He was telling me how nobody makes watches like that anymore, how everything is just cheap quartz movements now. He actually showed me the inside of an Apple Watch he was trying to fix for someone, and it's literally just a tiny computer board glued to a screen. There's no craftsmanship, you know? \n\nBut, uh... what were you asking? Oh, feeling happy. Yeah, I don't really have a good answer for that. I can't really think of a specific time recently. It's just all kind of a blur.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any moment in the past few weeks where you felt even a little bit of pleasure or satisfaction, even if it was something small, like finishing a task or having a good cup of coffee?\n\nPatient: Um... a moment of pleasure. Like a good cup of coffee. I mean, honestly, no, I can't really think of one. I wish I could. \n\nIt's funny you mention coffee, though, because the coffee in our breakroom is just an absolute nightmare. They buy these giant, industrial-sized plastic tubs of the cheapest grounds possible, and it always tastes like burnt dirt. And the machine itself... I swear it hasn't been cleaned since maybe 2018. If you open the top lid where the water goes, there's this thick white crust built up all over the spray head. I actually pointed it out to Ron the other day. He's the facility manager. Ron is one of those guys who constantly walks around the warehouse holding a clipboard just to look busy, but I have literally never seen him write a single thing on it. He just taps his pen against the clip while you talk to him. Anyway, I told him the coffee maker is probably a massive health hazard, and he just gave me this long speech about the quarterly budget. Like, the company can afford to switch our entire inventory system to that broken cloud software I was telling you about, but they can't spend forty bucks on a new Mr. Coffee at Target? It makes zero sense. \n\nBut, uh... wait. Did you ask about feeling satisfied? Yeah, sorry. No, I haven't really felt that way. Even with small stuff, it just hasn't really happened.\n\nDoctor: Has there been a time in the past two weeks when you felt pleasure or enjoyment, even for a short while, in something you used to like doing?\n\nPatient: Um... pleasure or enjoyment in something I used to like? I don't... I don't think so. Like, I used to really like working on my car, you know? Just tinkering with it on the weekends. But I haven't even opened the hood in weeks. \n\nSpeaking of my car, though, it's making this really weird sound when I turn the wheel. It's like a high-pitched squeal, almost like a belt slipping, but it only happens when I'm turning left at slow speeds. I took it to my usual mechanic, Dave. Dave's a great guy, he's got this massive golden retriever that just hangs out in the shop all day. The dog's name is Buster, and he'll literally bring you a wrench if you drop it. It's the craziest thing. Anyway, Dave looked at the power steering fluid, and he said it was fine, but he thinks it might be the power steering pump starting to go. He quoted me like four hundred bucks to fix it, which I definitely don't have right now, especially with how many hours they've been cutting at the warehouse. They brought in these new automated pallet jacks, and suddenly they don't need as many of us on the floor. It's just ridiculous. \n\nBut, uh... what was the question? Oh, enjoying things. Yeah, no, I haven't really felt like doing any of the stuff I used to like. I just don't have the energy for it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Um... enjoyment in something I used to like? I mean, you keep asking that, and I really wish I could give you a different answer, but no. Nothing really comes to mind. 

I used to really enjoy grilling out back, though. Like, smoking a brisket or something on a Sunday. But my grill is totally rusted out at the bottom now. I left the cover off during that huge storm we had last month—the one where the wind was so bad it blew over the neighbor's trampoline. It literally ended up in my driveway, wedged against my mailbox. The neighbor, Carl, he’s this guy who’s obsessed with his lawn. He mows it twice a week and edges the sidewalks with a weed whacker like he’s performing surgery. Anyway, Carl came over to get his trampoline and didn't even apologize for it hitting my mailbox. Just started complaining about how the wind ruined his freshly laid mulch. Like, I get it, Carl, but my mailbox is bent at a forty-five-degree angle now. I had to try and bend it back with a crowbar, and the mail carrier still gives me dirty looks when she drops off the mail. 

But, sorry, what did you ask again? Oh, right, feeling pleasure in stuff I used to like. Yeah, no, I just... I haven't had the drive to do anything like that lately. It's just not there.

They have these LED strips that change colors on holidays. It was green for St. Patrick's Day, which was kind of cool, but then they left it green for like a week after, and it just looked like a giant glowing glowstick across the water. \n\nBut, um... yeah, I don't know. I just don't have the energy for it. Even when Dave texts me about shooting hoops or grabbing a beer, I just tell him I'm busy. I'm not really busy, I just... I don't want to go. It just feels like too much effort to even pretend I'm having a good time.\n\nDoctor: Would you say you're feeling down or sad most of the time now, even when things aren't stressful at work?\n\nPatient: Um... down or sad most of the time? I mean, I don't really think so. Like I said before, I don't feel like I'm walking around crying or anything. It's not like a constant sadness. It's just... I don't know, it's more like a heaviness, I guess? But only really when I'm thinking about the warehouse. \n\nSpeaking of the warehouse, they just changed the whole inventory system on us again. It's this new software, supposed to be cloud-based or whatever. But the tablets they gave us to use it on are so old, the battery dies in like two hours. So you're constantly having to plug it in by the loading dock, and the cord is super short, so you have to stand there holding it while you scan the barcodes. And the barcodes themselves get all smudged from the forklift exhaust, so half the time the scanner won't even read them. You have to type in this twelve-digit SKU manually, and if you mess up one number, it throws off the whole manifest. My supervisor, Greg, he's always on my case about the scan rates, but I'm like, \"Greg, if you gave us equipment from this decade, maybe we could actually hit the numbers.\" He just shrugs. He's one of those guys who wears his sunglasses on the back of his neck, you know? Like, indoors. \n\nBut, uh... wait. Sad most of the time. No, I wouldn't say that. If work isn't on my mind, I'm not really sad. I'm just kind of... there. Like I'm just waiting for the next shift to start so I can get yelled at by Greg again.\n\nDoctor: Can you recall a time recently when you felt genuinely happy or satisfied, even for a little while, when work wasn’t on your mind?\n\nPatient: Um... genuinely happy or satisfied? I mean... I don't really know. I guess I haven't really been keeping track of, like, happy moments. It's hard to think of one right off the top of my head. \n\nI did find this old watch in a drawer the other day, though. It was my grandfather's, one of those mechanical ones you have to wind every day. It's got a leather strap that's practically falling apart, but the face is still in really good shape. It's a Bulova, I think from the sixties? I took it to this little repair shop downtown—it's tucked between a dry cleaner and a place that sells, like, discount vape juice. The guy in there is ancient, he wears these crazy magnifying glasses strapped to his head. He told me the mainspring was shot, but he could fix it if he could find the part. He was telling me how nobody makes watches like that anymore, how everything is just cheap quartz movements now. He actually showed me the inside of an Apple Watch he was trying to fix for someone, and it's literally just a tiny computer board glued to a screen. There's no craftsmanship, you know? \n\nBut, uh... what were you asking? Oh, feeling happy. Yeah, I don't really have a good answer for that. I can't really think of a specific time recently. It's just all kind of a blur.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any moment in the past few weeks where you felt even a little bit of pleasure or satisfaction, even if it was something small, like finishing a task or having a good cup of coffee?\n\nPatient: Um... a moment of pleasure. Like a good cup of coffee. I mean, honestly, no, I can't really think of one. I wish I could. \n\nIt's funny you mention coffee, though, because the coffee in our breakroom is just an absolute nightmare. They buy these giant, industrial-sized plastic tubs of the cheapest grounds possible, and it always tastes like burnt dirt. And the machine itself... I swear it hasn't been cleaned since maybe 2018. If you open the top lid where the water goes, there's this thick white crust built up all over the spray head. I actually pointed it out to Ron the other day. He's the facility manager. Ron is one of those guys who constantly walks around the warehouse holding a clipboard just to look busy, but I have literally never seen him write a single thing on it. He just taps his pen against the clip while you talk to him. Anyway, I told him the coffee maker is probably a massive health hazard, and he just gave me this long speech about the quarterly budget. Like, the company can afford to switch our entire inventory system to that broken cloud software I was telling you about, but they can't spend forty bucks on a new Mr. Coffee at Target? It makes zero sense. \n\nBut, uh... wait. Did you ask about feeling satisfied? Yeah, sorry. No, I haven't really felt that way. Even with small stuff, it just hasn't really happened.\n\nDoctor: Has there been a time in the past two weeks when you felt pleasure or enjoyment, even for a short while, in something you used to like doing?\n\nPatient: Um... pleasure or enjoyment in something I used to like? I don't... I don't think so. Like, I used to really like working on my car, you know? Just tinkering with it on the weekends. But I haven't even opened the hood in weeks. \n\nSpeaking of my car, though, it's making this really weird sound when I turn the wheel. It's like a high-pitched squeal, almost like a belt slipping, but it only happens when I'm turning left at slow speeds. I took it to my usual mechanic, Dave. Dave's a great guy, he's got this massive golden retriever that just hangs out in the shop all day. The dog's name is Buster, and he'll literally bring you a wrench if you drop it. It's the craziest thing. Anyway, Dave looked at the power steering fluid, and he said it was fine, but he thinks it might be the power steering pump starting to go. He quoted me like four hundred bucks to fix it, which I definitely don't have right now, especially with how many hours they've been cutting at the warehouse. They brought in these new automated pallet jacks, and suddenly they don't need as many of us on the floor. It's just ridiculous. \n\nBut, uh... what was the question? Oh, enjoying things. Yeah, no, I haven't really felt like doing any of the stuff I used to like. I just don't have the energy for it.\n\nDoctor: Has there been any time in the past two weeks when you felt even a little bit of pleasure or enjoyment in something you used to really like doing?\n\nPatient: Um... enjoyment in something I used to like? I mean, you keep asking that, and I really wish I could give you a different answer, but no. Nothing really comes to mind. \n\nI used to really enjoy grilling out back, though. Like, smoking a brisket or something on a Sunday. But my grill is totally rusted out at the bottom now. I left the cover off during that huge storm we had last month—the one where the wind was so bad it blew over the neighbor's trampoline. It literally ended up in my driveway, wedged against my mailbox. The neighbor, Carl, he’s this guy who’s obsessed with his lawn. He mows it twice a week and edges the sidewalks with a weed whacker like he’s performing surgery. Anyway, Carl came over to get his trampoline and didn't even apologize for it hitting my mailbox. Just started complaining about how the wind ruined his freshly laid mulch. Like, I get it, Carl, but my mailbox is bent at a forty-five-degree angle now. I had to try and bend it back with a crowbar, and the mail carrier still gives me dirty looks when she drops off the mail. \n\nBut, sorry, what did you ask again? Um... smiling or laughing about something not related to work? Man, I... I really can't remember. It's been a while since anything actually felt funny or made me want to smile. 

Actually, speaking of laughing, my sister called me the other day. She lives out in Arizona now, near Sedona. She bought this house that has a whole cactus garden in the front yard, which sounds cool until you realize how much maintenance it is. Anyway, she was telling me about this guy she started dating. He's a professional dog groomer, but only for show dogs. Like, poodles with the crazy haircuts and stuff. She said he spends like three hours just blow-drying a dog's ears. And the guy drives a bright yellow Hummer. Who even drives Hummers anymore? I feel like those went out of style in, what, 2005? The gas mileage on those things has to be atrocious, especially out in the desert. I remember my uncle had one back in the day, and he complained about filling up the tank literally every time I saw him. He used to keep a logbook in the glove compartment just to track his miles per gallon. 

But, uh... wait, what were we talking about? Smiling? Yeah, no, even when my sister was telling me about the dog groomer guy, I just kind of sat there. I didn't really laugh or anything. I just don't have it in me right now.

She lives out in Denver now, she works at this massive brewery out there. They make this stout that tastes like peanut butter, which sounds totally gross, right? And lately I just go home, sit down, look at the box, and... I don't even open it. I just end up going to bed. I don't know, I'm always kind of thinking about the warehouse anyway. \n\nLike, even this past Sunday, I was trying to just chill out and not think about it. I don't know, a light grey or something just to open the space up. But drywall is so expensive right now. I saw the prices in the lumber aisle and almost walked right out. He says the secret is roasting the tomatillos until they're almost burnt. I actually bought a cast-iron skillet a few months ago thinking I'd try it. You know, one of those heavy Lodge ones? They say you're not supposed to use soap on them, just scrub them with coarse salt, which sounds kind of unsanitary if you think about it. But my buddy Dave swears by it. He uses his for everything, even baking cornbread. I don't really get the appeal of cornbread, honestly. It's too dry. \n\nBut, uh, anyway... hanging out with friends? Like I said, I haven't been playing basketball. I don't really watch much TV either. I just kind of sit there. So, I don't know. I guess I'm not really finding a lot of enjoyment in stuff right now. Just the tacos, maybe.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you don't enjoy things as much as you used to, even things you really liked before, like sketching or hanging out with friends?\n\nPatient: I mean, yeah, I guess I just said that, right? I'm not really enjoying much of anything lately. It's weird, because I used to be able to just zone out with my sketchbook for hours. I'd go down to the riverfront... they just put in this new pedestrian bridge down there a couple years ago. It's supposed to look like a suspension bridge, but it's really just cable-stayed, you know? The city council made a huge deal about it, spent way too much money on the lighting design. They have these LED strips that change colors on holidays. It was green for St. Patrick's Day, which was kind of cool, but then they left it green for like a week after, and it just looked like a giant glowing glowstick across the water. \n\nBut, um... yeah, I don't know. I just don't have the energy for it. Even when Dave texts me about shooting hoops or grabbing a beer, I just tell him I'm busy. I'm not really busy, I just... I don't want to go. It just feels like too much effort to even pretend I'm having a good time.\n\nDoctor: Would you say you're feeling down or sad most of the time now, even when things aren't stressful at work?\n\nPatient: Um... down or sad most of the time? I mean, I don't really think so. Like I said before, I don't feel like I'm walking around crying or anything. It's not like a constant sadness. It's just... I don't know, it's more like a heaviness, I guess? But only really when I'm thinking about the warehouse. \n\nSpeaking of the warehouse, they just changed the whole inventory system on us again. It's this new software, supposed to be cloud-based or whatever. But the tablets they gave us to use it on are so old, the battery dies in like two hours. So you're constantly having to plug it in by the loading dock, and the cord is super short, so you have to stand there holding it while you scan the barcodes. And the barcodes themselves get all smudged from the forklift exhaust, so half the time the scanner won't even read them. You have to type in this twelve-digit SKU manually, and if you mess up one number, it throws off the whole manifest. My supervisor, Greg, he's always on my case about the scan rates, but I'm like, \"Greg, if you gave us equipment from this decade, maybe we could actually hit the numbers.\" He just shrugs. He's one of those guys who wears his sunglasses on the back of his neck, you know? Like, indoors. \n\nBut, uh... wait. Sad most of the time. No, I wouldn't say that. If work isn't on my mind, I'm not really sad. I'm just kind of... there. Like I'm just waiting for the next shift to start so I can get yelled at by Greg again.\n\nDoctor: Can you recall a time recently when you felt genuinely happy or satisfied, even for a little while, when work wasn’t on your mind?\n\nPatient: Um... genuinely happy or satisfied? I mean... I don't really know. I guess I haven't really been keeping track of, like, happy moments. It's hard to think of one right off the top of my head. \n\nI did find this old watch in a drawer the other day, though. It was my grandfather's, one of those mechanical ones you have to wind every day. It's got a leather strap that's practically falling apart, but the face is still in really good shape. It's a Bulova, I think from the sixties? I took it to this little repair shop downtown—it's tucked between a dry cleaner and a place that sells, like, discount vape juice. The guy in there is ancient, he wears these crazy magnifying glasses strapped to his head. He told me the mainspring was shot, but he could fix it if he could find the part. He was telling me how nobody makes watches like that anymore, how everything is just cheap quartz movements now. He actually showed me the inside of an Apple Watch he was trying to fix for someone, and it's literally just a tiny computer board glued to a screen. There's no craftsmanship, you know? \n\nBut, uh... what were you asking? Oh, feeling happy. Yeah, I don't really have a good answer for that. I can't really think of a specific time recently. It's just all kind of a blur.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any moment in the past few weeks where you felt even a little bit of pleasure or satisfaction, even if it was something small, like finishing a task or having a good cup of coffee?\n\nPatient: Um... a moment of pleasure. Like a good cup of coffee. I mean, honestly, no, I can't really think of one. I wish I could. \n\nIt's funny you mention coffee, though, because the coffee in our breakroom is just an absolute nightmare. They buy these giant, industrial-sized plastic tubs of the cheapest grounds possible, and it always tastes like burnt dirt. And the machine itself... I swear it hasn't been cleaned since maybe 2018. If you open the top lid where the water goes, there's this thick white crust built up all over the spray head. I actually pointed it out to Ron the other day. He's the facility manager. Ron is one of those guys who constantly walks around the warehouse holding a clipboard just to look busy, but I have literally never seen him write a single thing on it. He just taps his pen against the clip while you talk to him. Anyway, I told him the coffee maker is probably a massive health hazard, and he just gave me this long speech about the quarterly budget. Like, the company can afford to switch our entire inventory system to that broken cloud software I was telling you about, but they can't spend forty bucks on a new Mr. Coffee at Target? It makes zero sense. \n\nBut, uh... wait. Did you ask about feeling satisfied? Yeah, sorry. No, I haven't really felt that way. Even with small stuff, it just hasn't really happened.\n\nDoctor: Has there been a time in the past two weeks when you felt pleasure or enjoyment, even for a short while, in something you used to like doing?\n\nPatient: Um... pleasure or enjoyment in something I used to like? I don't... I don't think so. Like, I used to really like working on my car, you know? Just tinkering with it on the weekends. But I haven't even opened the hood in weeks. \n\nSpeaking of my car, though, it's making this really weird sound when I turn the wheel. It's like a high-pitched squeal, almost like a belt slipping, but it only happens when I'm turning left at slow speeds. I took it to my usual mechanic, Dave. Dave's a great guy, he's got this massive golden retriever that just hangs out in the shop all day. The dog's name is Buster, and he'll literally bring you a wrench if you drop it. It's the craziest thing. Anyway, Dave looked at the power steering fluid, and he said it was fine, but he thinks it might be the power steering pump starting to go. He quoted me like four hundred bucks to fix it, which I definitely don't have right now, especially with how many hours they've been cutting at the warehouse. They brought in these new automated pallet jacks, and suddenly they don't need as many of us on the floor. It's just ridiculous. \n\nBut, uh... what was the question? Oh, enjoying things. Yeah, no, I haven't really felt like doing any of the stuff I used to like. I just don't have the energy for it.\n\nDoctor: Has there been any time in the past two weeks when you felt even a little bit of pleasure or enjoyment in something you used to really like doing?\n\nPatient: Um... enjoyment in something I used to like? I mean, you keep asking that, and I really wish I could give you a different answer, but no. Nothing really comes to mind. \n\nI used to really enjoy grilling out back, though. Like, smoking a brisket or something on a Sunday. But my grill is totally rusted out at the bottom now. I left the cover off during that huge storm we had last month—the one where the wind was so bad it blew over the neighbor's trampoline. It literally ended up in my driveway, wedged against my mailbox. The neighbor, Carl, he’s this guy who’s obsessed with his lawn. He mows it twice a week and edges the sidewalks with a weed whacker like he’s performing surgery. Anyway, Carl came over to get his trampoline and didn't even apologize for it hitting my mailbox. Just started complaining about how the wind ruined his freshly laid mulch. Like, I get it, Carl, but my mailbox is bent at a forty-five-degree angle now. I had to try and bend it back with a crowbar, and the mail carrier still gives me dirty looks when she drops off the mail. \n\nBut, sorry, what did you ask again? Oh, right, feeling pleasure in stuff I used to like. Yeah, no, I just... I haven't had the drive to do anything like that lately. It's just not there.\n\nDoctor: Can you remember the last time you smiled or laughed, even just a little, about something that wasn’t related to work or stress?\n\nPatient: Um... smiling or laughing about something not related to work? Man, I... I really can't remember. It's been a while since anything actually felt funny or made me want to smile. \n\nActually, speaking of laughing, my sister called me the other day. She lives out in Arizona now, near Sedona. She bought this house that has a whole cactus garden in the front yard, which sounds cool until you realize how much maintenance it is. Anyway, she was telling me about this guy she started dating. He's a professional dog groomer, but only for show dogs. Like, poodles with the crazy haircuts and stuff. She said he spends like three hours just blow-drying a dog's ears. And the guy drives a bright yellow Hummer. Who even drives Hummers anymore? I feel like those went out of style in, what, 2005? The gas mileage on those things has to be atrocious, especially out in the desert. I remember my uncle had one back in the day, and he complained about filling up the tank literally every time I saw him. He used to keep a logbook in the glove compartment just to track his miles per gallon. \n\nBut, uh... wait, what were we talking about? Smiling? Yeah, no, even when my sister was telling me about the dog groomer guy, I just kind of sat there. I didn't really laugh or anything. I just don't have it in me right now.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Um... pleasure or interest in things I used to enjoy? I feel like we're going in circles here, doc. No, I really haven't. Not even for a minute. 

I used to be really into building those model airplanes, though. You know, the World War II ones? People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there.     "role": "user",

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Um... my mood being low even when work is okay? Yeah, I mean, even when I'm not thinking about the warehouse, I just feel this... emptiness, I guess. It doesn't matter what I'm doing. 

Like yesterday, I was just sitting on the porch watching the cars go by. We live on this corner where people always take the turn way too fast. There's this one guy in a silver Mustang—he's got one of those loud exhausts that sounds like a gunshot when it backfires. He comes flying around that corner every afternoon right around 4:30. I'm pretty sure he works at the auto parts store down the street, because I saw his car parked there when I went to get new wiper blades last winter. The guy at the counter tried to sell me on these "premium" silicone blades that were like forty bucks each. Forty bucks! For wiper blades! I mean, I get that you need to see when it rains, but the regular rubber ones have been working fine for, what, a hundred years? I ended up just buying the cheap ones, and they squeak a little when it's just misting, but whatever. 

But, uh, what was the question? Oh, right, feeling empty. Yeah, even when I was just sitting there watching the cars, not even thinking about my hours getting cut, I just felt really down. Like there's just a hole there, you know? It never really goes away right now.

Like I'm just waiting for the next shift to start so I can get yelled at by Greg again.\n\nDoctor: Can you recall a time recently when you felt genuinely happy or satisfied, even for a little while, when work wasn’t on your mind?\n\nPatient: Um... genuinely happy or satisfied? I mean... I don't really know. I guess I haven't really been keeping track of, like, happy moments. It's hard to think of one right off the top of my head. \n\nI did find this old watch in a drawer the other day, though. It was my grandfather's, one of those mechanical ones you have to wind every day. It's got a leather strap that's practically falling apart, but the face is still in really good shape. It's a Bulova, I think from the sixties? I took it to this little repair shop downtown—it's tucked between a dry cleaner and a place that sells, like, discount vape juice. The guy in there is ancient, he wears these crazy magnifying glasses strapped to his head. He told me the mainspring was shot, but he could fix it if he could find the part. He was telling me how nobody makes watches like that anymore, how everything is just cheap quartz movements now. He actually showed me the inside of an Apple Watch he was trying to fix for someone, and it's literally just a tiny computer board glued to a screen. There's no craftsmanship, you know? \n\nBut, uh... what were you asking? Oh, feeling happy. Yeah, I don't really have a good answer for that. I can't really think of a specific time recently. It's just all kind of a blur.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any moment in the past few weeks where you felt even a little bit of pleasure or satisfaction, even if it was something small, like finishing a task or having a good cup of coffee?\n\nPatient: Um... a moment of pleasure. Like a good cup of coffee. I mean, honestly, no, I can't really think of one. I wish I could. \n\nIt's funny you mention coffee, though, because the coffee in our breakroom is just an absolute nightmare. They buy these giant, industrial-sized plastic tubs of the cheapest grounds possible, and it always tastes like burnt dirt. And the machine itself... I swear it hasn't been cleaned since maybe 2018. If you open the top lid where the water goes, there's this thick white crust built up all over the spray head. I actually pointed it out to Ron the other day. He's the facility manager. Ron is one of those guys who constantly walks around the warehouse holding a clipboard just to look busy, but I have literally never seen him write a single thing on it. He just taps his pen against the clip while you talk to him. Anyway, I told him the coffee maker is probably a massive health hazard, and he just gave me this long speech about the quarterly budget. Like, the company can afford to switch our entire inventory system to that broken cloud software I was telling you about, but they can't spend forty bucks on a new Mr. Coffee at Target? It makes zero sense. \n\nBut, uh... wait. Did you ask about feeling satisfied? Yeah, sorry. No, I haven't really felt that way. Even with small stuff, it just hasn't really happened.\n\nDoctor: Has there been a time in the past two weeks when you felt pleasure or enjoyment, even for a short while, in something you used to like doing?\n\nPatient: Um... pleasure or enjoyment in something I used to like? I don't... I don't think so. Like, I used to really like working on my car, you know? Just tinkering with it on the weekends. But I haven't even opened the hood in weeks. \n\nSpeaking of my car, though, it's making this really weird sound when I turn the wheel. It's like a high-pitched squeal, almost like a belt slipping, but it only happens when I'm turning left at slow speeds. I took it to my usual mechanic, Dave. Dave's a great guy, he's got this massive golden retriever that just hangs out in the shop all day. The dog's name is Buster, and he'll literally bring you a wrench if you drop it. It's the craziest thing. Anyway, Dave looked at the power steering fluid, and he said it was fine, but he thinks it might be the power steering pump starting to go. He quoted me like four hundred bucks to fix it, which I definitely don't have right now, especially with how many hours they've been cutting at the warehouse. They brought in these new automated pallet jacks, and suddenly they don't need as many of us on the floor. It's just ridiculous. \n\nBut, uh... what was the question? Oh, enjoying things. Yeah, no, I haven't really felt like doing any of the stuff I used to like. I just don't have the energy for it.\n\nDoctor: Has there been any time in the past two weeks when you felt even a little bit of pleasure or enjoyment in something you used to really like doing?\n\nPatient: Um... enjoyment in something I used to like? I mean, you keep asking that, and I really wish I could give you a different answer, but no. Nothing really comes to mind. \n\nI used to really enjoy grilling out back, though. Like, smoking a brisket or something on a Sunday. But my grill is totally rusted out at the bottom now. I left the cover off during that huge storm we had last month—the one where the wind was so bad it blew over the neighbor's trampoline. It literally ended up in my driveway, wedged against my mailbox. The neighbor, Carl, he’s this guy who’s obsessed with his lawn. He mows it twice a week and edges the sidewalks with a weed whacker like he’s performing surgery. Anyway, Carl came over to get his trampoline and didn't even apologize for it hitting my mailbox. Just started complaining about how the wind ruined his freshly laid mulch. Like, I get it, Carl, but my mailbox is bent at a forty-five-degree angle now. I had to try and bend it back with a crowbar, and the mail carrier still gives me dirty looks when she drops off the mail. \n\nBut, sorry, what did you ask again? Oh, right, feeling pleasure in stuff I used to like. Yeah, no, I just... I haven't had the drive to do anything like that lately. It's just not there.\n\nDoctor: Can you remember the last time you smiled or laughed, even just a little, about something that wasn’t related to work or stress?\n\nPatient: Um... smiling or laughing about something not related to work? Man, I... I really can't remember. It's been a while since anything actually felt funny or made me want to smile. \n\nActually, speaking of laughing, my sister called me the other day. She lives out in Arizona now, near Sedona. She bought this house that has a whole cactus garden in the front yard, which sounds cool until you realize how much maintenance it is. Anyway, she was telling me about this guy she started dating. He's a professional dog groomer, but only for show dogs. Like, poodles with the crazy haircuts and stuff. She said he spends like three hours just blow-drying a dog's ears. And the guy drives a bright yellow Hummer. Who even drives Hummers anymore? I feel like those went out of style in, what, 2005? The gas mileage on those things has to be atrocious, especially out in the desert. I remember my uncle had one back in the day, and he complained about filling up the tank literally every time I saw him. He used to keep a logbook in the glove compartment just to track his miles per gallon. \n\nBut, uh... wait, what were we talking about? Smiling? Yeah, no, even when my sister was telling me about the dog groomer guy, I just kind of sat there. I didn't really laugh or anything. Like baked chips and granola bars that taste like sawdust. The old guy who stocks the machines, Frank, he was so mad about it. He said the corporate office forced them to switch suppliers. Frank's been there for like twenty years, and he always used to sneak me an extra Snickers bar when he was refilling the rows. Now he just looks depressed when he's loading in the veggie straws. \n\nBut, sorry, I'm rambling again. What did you ask? Oh, right, interest in things. Yeah, no, the model airplane is just sitting there gathering dust. I look at it and I just feel... nothing. I don't care about finishing it at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that your mood feels low or empty even when things at work are going okay, or when you’re not thinking about work at all?\n\nPatient: Um... my mood being low even when work is okay? Wait, didn't we... didn't you just ask me that? I swear we just went over the whole model airplane thing a minute ago. Or maybe my brain is just totally fried today.

And lately I just go home, sit down, look at the box, and... I don't even open it. I just end up going to bed. So I'm not really sure if the interest is gone or if my brain is just completely fried from the warehouse.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe your mood throughout the day, especially when you’re not at work or thinking about work? Do you feel down or sad even when there’s no specific reason for it?\n\nPatient: Um... my mood when I'm not at work? I don't really know if I feel sad, exactly. Like, if there's no reason for it, I don't think I'm just walking around crying or anything. It's more just... I don't know, I'm always kind of thinking about the warehouse anyway. \n\nLike, even this past Sunday, I was trying to just chill out and not think about it. I went to Home Depot to get some weather stripping for my back door because the draft in my kitchen is getting ridiculous. My house was built in the 70s, and I swear the insulation is just garbage. You know those houses with the weird, dark wood paneling everywhere? Yeah, my whole basement is covered in it. I've been meaning to rip it out for years, maybe put up some drywall, paint it... I don't know, a light grey or something just to open the space up. But drywall is so expensive right now. I saw the prices in the lumber aisle and almost walked right out. The guy working there was zero help, too, he just kept looking at his phone. \n\nBut, um... wait, what were you asking? Oh, feeling down. Yeah, I haven't really noticed feeling sad for no specific reason. I just feel kind of blank when I get home.\n\nDoctor: When you’re not at work, do you still find enjoyment in other things, like hanging out with friends, watching TV, or even small things like a good meal or a comfortable bed?\n\nPatient: Um... enjoyment in other things? I mean, I guess I still like a good meal. Like, there's this taco truck that parks near the warehouse on Thursdays. Best al pastor I've ever had. They have this green salsa that is just... man, it's got a kick. The guy who runs it, Hector, he's always telling me I need to try making it at home. He says the secret is roasting the tomatillos until they're almost burnt. I actually bought a cast-iron skillet a few months ago thinking I'd try it. You know, one of those heavy Lodge ones? They say you're not supposed to use soap on them, just scrub them with coarse salt, which sounds kind of unsanitary if you think about it. But my buddy Dave swears by it. He uses his for everything, even baking cornbread. I don't really get the appeal of cornbread, honestly. It's too dry. \n\nBut, uh, anyway... hanging out with friends? Like I said, I haven't been playing basketball. I don't really watch much TV either. I just kind of sit there. So, I don't know. I guess I'm not really finding a lot of enjoyment in stuff right now. Just the tacos, maybe.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you don't enjoy things as much as you used to, even things you really liked before, like sketching or hanging out with friends?\n\nPatient: I mean, yeah, I guess I just said that, right? I'm not really enjoying much of anything lately. It's weird, because I used to be able to just zone out with my sketchbook for hours. I'd go down to the riverfront... they just put in this new pedestrian bridge down there a couple years ago. It's supposed to look like a suspension bridge, but it's really just cable-stayed, you know? The city council made a huge deal about it, spent way too much money on the lighting design. They have these LED strips that change colors on holidays. It was green for St. Patrick's Day, which was kind of cool, but then they left it green for like a week after, and it just looked like a giant glowing glowstick across the water. \n\nBut, um... yeah, I don't know. I just don't have the energy for it. Even when Dave texts me about shooting hoops or grabbing a beer, I just tell him I'm busy. I'm not really busy, I just... I don't want to go. It just feels like too much effort to even pretend I'm having a good time.\n\nDoctor: Would you say you're feeling down or sad most of the time now, even when things aren't stressful at work?\n\nPatient: Um... down or sad most of the time? I mean, I don't really think so. Like I said before, I don't feel like I'm walking around crying or anything. It's not like a constant sadness. It's just... I don't know, it's more like a heaviness, I guess? But only really when I'm thinking about the warehouse. \n\nSpeaking of the warehouse, they just changed the whole inventory system on us again. It's this new software, supposed to be cloud-based or whatever. But the tablets they gave us to use it on are so old, the battery dies in like two hours. So you're constantly having to plug it in by the loading dock, and the cord is super short, so you have to stand there holding it while you scan the barcodes. And the barcodes themselves get all smudged from the forklift exhaust, so half the time the scanner won't even read them. You have to type in this twelve-digit SKU manually, and if you mess up one number, it throws off the whole manifest. My supervisor, Greg, he's always on my case about the scan rates, but I'm like, \"Greg, if you gave us equipment from this decade, maybe we could actually hit the numbers.\" He just shrugs. He's one of those guys who wears his sunglasses on the back of his neck, you know? Like, indoors. \n\nBut, uh... wait. Sad most of the time. No, I wouldn't say that. If work isn't on my mind, I'm not really sad. I'm just kind of... there. Like I'm just waiting for the next shift to start so I can get yelled at by Greg again.\n\nDoctor: Can you recall a time recently when you felt genuinely happy or satisfied, even for a little while, when work wasn’t on your mind?\n\nPatient: Um... genuinely happy or satisfied? I mean... I don't really know. I guess I haven't really been keeping track of, like, happy moments. It's hard to think of one right off the top of my head. \n\nI did find this old watch in a drawer the other day, though. It was my grandfather's, one of those mechanical ones you have to wind every day. It's got a leather strap that's practically falling apart, but the face is still in really good shape. It's a Bulova, I think from the sixties? I took it to this little repair shop downtown—it's tucked between a dry cleaner and a place that sells, like, discount vape juice. The guy in there is ancient, he wears these crazy magnifying glasses strapped to his head. He told me the mainspring was shot, but he could fix it if he could find the part. He was telling me how nobody makes watches like that anymore, how everything is just cheap quartz movements now. He actually showed me the inside of an Apple Watch he was trying to fix for someone, and it's literally just a tiny computer board glued to a screen. There's no craftsmanship, you know? \n\nBut, uh... what were you asking? Oh, feeling happy. Yeah, I don't really have a good answer for that. I can't really think of a specific time recently. It's just all kind of a blur.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any moment in the past few weeks where you felt even a little bit of pleasure or satisfaction, even if it was something small, like finishing a task or having a good cup of coffee?\n\nPatient: Um... a moment of pleasure. Like a good cup of coffee. I mean, honestly, no, I can't really think of one. I wish I could. \n\nIt's funny you mention coffee, though, because the coffee in our breakroom is just an absolute nightmare. They buy these giant, industrial-sized plastic tubs of the cheapest grounds possible, and it always tastes like burnt dirt. And the machine itself... I swear it hasn't been cleaned since maybe 2018. If you open the top lid where the water goes, there's this thick white crust built up all over the spray head. I actually pointed it out to Ron the other day. He's the facility manager. Ron is one of those guys who constantly walks around the warehouse holding a clipboard just to look busy, but I have literally never seen him write a single thing on it. He just taps his pen against the clip while you talk to him. Anyway, I told him the coffee maker is probably a massive health hazard, and he just gave me this long speech about the quarterly budget. Like, the company can afford to switch our entire inventory system to that broken cloud software I was telling you about, but they can't spend forty bucks on a new Mr. Coffee at Target? It makes zero sense. \n\nBut, uh... wait. Did you ask about feeling satisfied? Yeah, sorry. No, I haven't really felt that way. Even with small stuff, it just hasn't really happened.\n\nDoctor: Has there been a time in the past two weeks when you felt pleasure or enjoyment, even for a short while, in something you used to like doing?\n\nPatient: Um... pleasure or enjoyment in something I used to like? I don't... I don't think so. Like, I used to really like working on my car, you know? Just tinkering with it on the weekends. But I haven't even opened the hood in weeks. \n\nSpeaking of my car, though, it's making this really weird sound when I turn the wheel. It's like a high-pitched squeal, almost like a belt slipping, but it only happens when I'm turning left at slow speeds. I took it to my usual mechanic, Dave. Dave's a great guy, he's got this massive golden retriever that just hangs out in the shop all day. The dog's name is Buster, and he'll literally bring you a wrench if you drop it. It's the craziest thing. Anyway, Dave looked at the power steering fluid, and he said it was fine, but he thinks it might be the power steering pump starting to go. He quoted me like four hundred bucks to fix it, which I definitely don't have right now, especially with how many hours they've been cutting at the warehouse. They brought in these new automated pallet jacks, and suddenly they don't need as many of us on the floor. It's just ridiculous. \n\nBut, uh... what was the question? Oh, enjoying things. Yeah, no, I haven't really felt like doing any of the stuff I used to like. I just don't have the energy for it.\n\nDoctor: Has there been any time in the past two weeks when you felt even a little bit of pleasure or enjoyment in something you used to really like doing?\n\nPatient: Um... enjoyment in something I used to like? I mean, you keep asking that, and I really wish I could give you a different answer, but no. Nothing really comes to mind. \n\nI used to really enjoy grilling out back, though. Like, smoking a brisket or something on a Sunday. But my grill is totally rusted out at the bottom now. I left the cover off during that huge storm we had last month—the one where the wind was so bad it blew over the neighbor's trampoline. It literally ended up in my driveway, wedged against my mailbox. The neighbor, Carl, he’s this guy who’s obsessed with his lawn. He mows it twice a week and edges the sidewalks with a weed whacker like he’s performing surgery. Anyway, Carl came over to get his trampoline and didn't even apologize for it hitting my mailbox. Just started complaining about how the wind ruined his freshly laid mulch. Like, I get it, Carl, but my mailbox is bent at a forty-five-degree angle now. I had to try and bend it back with a crowbar, and the mail carrier still gives me dirty looks when she drops off the mail. \n\nBut, sorry, what did you ask again? Oh, right, feeling pleasure in stuff I used to like. Yeah, no, I just... I haven't had the drive to do anything like that lately. It's just not there.\n\nDoctor: Can you remember the last time you smiled or laughed, even just a little, about something that wasn’t related to work or stress?\n\nPatient: Um... smiling or laughing about something not related to work? Man, I... I really can't remember. It's been a while since anything actually felt funny or made me want to smile. \n\nActually, speaking of laughing, my sister called me the other day. She lives out in Arizona now, near Sedona. She bought this house that has a whole cactus garden in the front yard, which sounds cool until you realize how much maintenance it is. Anyway, she was telling me about this guy she started dating. He's a professional dog groomer, but only for show dogs. Like, poodles with the crazy haircuts and stuff. She said he spends like three hours just blow-drying a dog's ears. And the guy drives a bright yellow Hummer. Who even drives Hummers anymore? I feel like those went out of style in, what, 2005? The gas mileage on those things has to be atrocious, especially out in the desert. I remember my uncle had one back in the day, and he complained about filling up the tank literally every time I saw him. He used to keep a logbook in the glove compartment just to track his miles per gallon. \n\nBut, uh... wait, what were we talking about? Smiling? Yeah, no, even when my sister was telling me about the dog groomer guy, I just kind of sat there. I didn't really laugh or anything. I just don't have it in me right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt any pleasure or interest in activities you used to enjoy, even for a short moment, in the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Um... pleasure or interest in things I used to enjoy? I feel like we're going in circles here, doc. No, I really haven't. Not even for a minute. \n\nI used to be really into building those model airplanes, though. You know, the World War II ones? I had this whole setup in the spare bedroom with the tiny paintbrushes and the special glue that smells like, I don't know, toxic apples or something. I was working on a B-17 Flying Fortress for months. The detail on those things is insane—you literally have to paint the little dials inside the cockpit, even though no one is ever going to see them once you glue the canopy on. It's wild. But anyway, I haven't touched it since the warehouse cut my hours. Speaking of the warehouse, they just changed the vending machines in the break room, and now it's all this \"healthy\" stuff. Like baked chips and granola bars that taste like sawdust. The old guy who stocks the machines, Frank, he was so mad about it. He said the corporate office forced them to switch suppliers. Frank's been there for like twenty years, and he always used to sneak me an extra Snickers bar when he was refilling the rows. Now he just looks depressed when he's loading in the veggie straws. \n\nBut, sorry, I'm rambling again. What did you ask? Oh, right, interest in things. Yeah, no, the model airplane is just sitting there gathering dust. I look at it and I just feel... nothing. I don't care about finishing it at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that your mood feels low or empty even when things at work are going okay, or when you’re not thinking about work at all?\n\nPatient: Um... my mood being low even when work is okay? Yeah, I mean, even when I'm not thinking about the warehouse, I just feel this... emptiness, I guess. It doesn't matter what I'm doing. \n\nLike yesterday, I was just sitting on the porch watching the cars go by. We live on this corner where people always take the turn way too fast. There's this one guy in a silver Mustang—he's got one of those loud exhausts that sounds like a gunshot when it backfires. He comes flying around that corner every afternoon right around 4:30. I'm pretty sure he works at the auto parts store down the street, because I saw his car parked there when I went to get new wiper blades last winter. The guy at the counter tried to sell me on these \"premium\" silicone blades that were like forty bucks each. Forty bucks! For wiper blades! I mean, I get that you need to see when it rains, but the regular rubber ones have been working fine for, what, a hundred years? I ended up just buying the cheap ones, and they squeak a little when it's just misting, but whatever. \n\nBut, uh, what was the question? Oh, right, feeling empty. Yeah, even when I was just sitting there watching the cars, not even thinking about my hours getting cut, I just felt really down. Like there's just a hole there, you know? It never really goes away right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt any pleasure or interest in activities you used to enjoy, even for a short moment, in the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Wait, didn't we... didn't you just ask me that? I swear we just went over the whole model airplane thing a minute ago. Or maybe my brain is just totally fried today. \n\nSpeaking of fried brains, they're making us do these massive cycle counts at the warehouse right now, and it is a nightmare. They brought in these brand new handheld barcode scanners that are supposed to make everything, you know, streamlined. But the screens are so small you can barely read the SKUs, and the battery dies after like two hours. My buddy Dave—he drives one of the stand-up forklifts—he accidentally dropped one of the new scanners right into the cardboard baler on Tuesday. Crushed it completely flat. The floor supervisor was absolutely losing his mind, screaming about how they cost a thousand bucks each. A thousand bucks for a piece of plastic that can't even hold a charge! It's ridiculous. Dave thought he was gonna get fired on the spot, but they just gave him a final written warning. \n\nBut, uh... yeah, sorry. You asked about enjoying things again, right? My answer's still the same, doc. I really haven't felt any pleasure or interest in anything. It's just been completely flat for the last couple of weeks.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. However, the symptoms appear to be occurring in response to identifiable stressors (e.g., increased work demands, role changes, interpersonal issues at work) and do not yet clearly meet full criteria for a major depressive episode. F32.1 (Moderate depressive episode) remains a consideration given the duration and severity of mood symptoms, while F43.22 (Adjustment disorder with depressed mood) remains relevant due to the temporal link with psychosocial stressors. No new disorder has been confirmed or ruled out definitively at this time.", "is_final": false}
